Extremely strange or impossible? Heavens, no. I don't believe in fairy tales, doctor. I'm a pragmatic woman. You don't survive twenty-five years—well, twenty-seven if you count my time as a junior coordinator before I went back for my bachelor's—managing an administrative department by indulging in the impossible. Everything I suspect... no, let me correct that, everything I *know* is happening with my sister's care is entirely possible, and frankly, very common if you actually know where to look. 

I'm not talking about, you know, government agents beaming signals through my television set or aliens or microchips in the water supply or whatever it is you're checking for. If one of my staff came to me with that kind of nonsense, I'd send them straight to HR! What I'm dealing with is basic, run-of-the-mill, bureaucratic obstruction. It's just human nature. People make mistakes, they realize they've messed up the billing codes or violated a scheduling protocol, and instead of admitting fault and facing the consequences, they try to cover it up. And then they form these little alliances to keep the person asking the hard questions—which is me—out of the loop. 

So, no, there is nothing impossible about it. It’s all math, logistics, and office politics. Now, I will admit, my sister's neighbor, Susan—she means well, but she's not very sharp, bless her heart—she told me I was overthinking it. She said it was 'impossible' for a whole clinic staff to coordinate against one person just to hide some paperwork. But I just had to laugh, because I told her, 'Susan, you have clearly never seen a department head try to hide a budget deficit right before a quarterly audit!' It is very, very possible. I've seen people do much more for much less. 

If anyone thinks my suspicions are strange, it’s only because they haven't looked at my documentation. They haven't done the work. I have the emails printed out. I have the call logs cross-referenced with the exact dates and times they claimed they tried to reach me, which proves they didn't. Nothing I'm saying defies the laws of physics or logic. It’s just plain, undeniable fact, meticulously cataloged. So if you're asking if I believe in things that can't possibly happen... no. I only believe in what the paper trail proves, and my paper trail is airtight.

Are they trying to cover up some sort of massive insurance fraud by making me look crazy so I can't blow the whistle? Because I *have* caught discrepancies in the billing, like I told you. Or is it more personal? Is there someone at the clinic who knows someone I fired years ago and they're holding a grudge? Or maybe they just don't like that I ask questions. You know how some doctors—present company excluded, of course—but some doctors and office managers, they just want patients who nod and say 'yes, doctor' and don't challenge them. And I challenge them. I ask for the itemized receipts. I demand to know why a specialist referral takes three weeks when the standard turnaround time in my office is 48 hours. \n\nSo, do I know their exact, specific motive? No. I don't have the smoking gun yet. I don't have the one piece of paper that says 'Target her because of X, Y, and Z.' But I know there *is* a motive. People don't coordinate this level of interference just for fun. It takes time, it takes effort to mess with someone's life this consistently. They have a reason, doctor. I just... I haven't uncovered the exact nature of it yet. But I will. I'm very thorough.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was significantly different than usual—like feeling unusually down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you normally enjoy, or feeling unusually energized, restless, or overly confident?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, I know you have to ask these things, it's part of the standard intake questionnaire, I'm very familiar with the process. But no, my mood is perfectly stable. I haven't been depressed or down in the dumps, and I certainly haven't been bouncing off the walls with energy or feeling overly confident, as you put it. \n\nHonestly, doctor, how could I possibly be depressed when I have so much work to do? I don't have the luxury of losing interest in things. I have a whole administrative team relying on me at the office, making sure the schedules are tight and the protocols are followed. And then I come home, and I have my sister's care to manage. I have the binder to update. I have the billing statements to cross-reference with the appointment logs. If anything, I'm just focused. Highly, highly focused. \n\nPeople might look at me and think, \"Oh, she's stressed, she's working too hard, she must be exhausted.\" And sure, I get tired, anyone would get tired dealing with this level of incompetence—or, well, intentional sabotage, as we've discussed. But tired is not the same as depressed. Tired is just a biological response to being the only person willing to put in the hours to uncover what's really going on. And as for feeling overly energized or restless... well, I'm thorough. I'm a thorough person. If I stay up late organizing my documentation, it's not because I'm manic or something, it's because the documentation needs to be organized. It's because if I don't do it, they win. They slip another charge past me, they cancel another appointment, and they get one step closer to pushing me out. \n\nSo, no. My mood is exactly what it should be for someone in my position. I'm alert, I'm diligent, and I'm entirely focused on the task at hand. There hasn't been any sort of extreme shift in my emotions, just a very necessary shift in my priorities to deal with this situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or sensed things that others around you don’t seem to notice, even when you were certain they were real at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, if you're asking if I'm hallucinating—seeing ghosts or hearing voices whispering in my ear—no, absolutely not. I know that's what that question means. I've read the DSM criteria, just out of curiosity, you understand. I like to be informed. But no, I don't see or hear things that aren't there. Everything I've noticed, everything I've documented in my binder, is entirely real and grounded in reality. \n\nI mean, I suppose you could say I notice things that *other* people don't seem to notice, but that's not because I'm imagining things. It's because other people aren't paying attention! People walk through life with blinders on, doctor. They just accept things at face value. A receptionist says, 'Oh, the computer system is down, we lost your appointment,' and the average person just sighs and reschedules. But I see the pattern. I see the receptionist looking away, avoiding eye contact, I see the slight hesitation before she types. I notice the way the clinic manager happens to walk by the desk exactly when I'm asking for a copy of the itemized bill. \n\nSo, do I sense things others don't? Yes, but it's a heightened sense of observation, not a... a sensory hallucination. It's my administrative training kicking in. When you spend thirty years managing an office, you learn to read people. You learn to spot the subtle cues when someone is lying to you or trying to cover their tracks. You notice when the tone of an email is just slightly off, or when a phone call is routed to a different department than it usually is. \n\nFor instance, last Thursday—or was it Wednesday? No, Thursday, because it was trash day—I was on the phone with the insurance company, and I could hear a faint clicking sound on the line. Now, most people would just think, 'Oh, a bad connection.' But I know what a secure line sounds like, and I know what interference sounds like. I didn't *imagine* the click. It was a real, physical sound. But I was the only one who recognized its significance. I was the only one who realized they were probably recording the call or monitoring my inquiries. \n\nSo, no, I don't have hallucinations. I don't see things that aren't there. I just see the things that *are* there, the things that everyone else is too busy or too naive to notice. I see the connections. And honestly, it's a good thing I do, because if I weren't paying this close attention, they would have gotten away with this months ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that certain events, people, or everyday occurrences have a special or hidden meaning specifically for you, even if others don’t see it that way?\n\nPatient: Well, \"hidden meaning\" makes it sound like I'm reading tea leaves or looking for signs in the clouds, doctor, and I'm a practical woman. I deal in facts, in logistics, in documentation. But do I believe these everyday occurrences mean something more than what people claim they do? Absolutely. Because they do. \n\nI’m here because for months now—literally months, going back to at least early fall, which I have meticulously tracked—I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern. And it is a pattern, not a coincidence. Take my sister’s care, for example. I manage everything for her, as I've mentioned. But suddenly, out of nowhere, her appointments get moved without my authorization. They’ll just change a Tuesday morning specialist slot to a Thursday afternoon and claim they sent an automated text, which, of course, I never received. And then the financial side... the bills show up wrong over and over again. Double charges for basic consultations, incorrect diagnostic codes that I have to personally audit. \n\nAnd when I call to get it sorted out? When I try to hold them accountable? People just conveniently \"forget\" to call me back. I leave detailed, professional messages with the front desk, with the billing coordinator, with the clinic manager, and it's just crickets. Days will go by. \n\nNow, if you ask them, or if you ask anyone else looking at this from the outside, everyone says it’s just mistakes. \"Oh, we're short-staffed, the new software system updated, it's just a clerical error, we're so sorry.\" They expect me to just swallow that excuse. But I've supervised administrative support workers for decades. I know the statistical probability of this many \"mistakes\" happening to one single patient account, and it is practically zero. \n\nI know what's really happening. I know they’re intentionally keeping information from me. It's a concerted effort. They want to keep me in the dark and try to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. They know I ask the hard questions, so they think if they make it difficult enough, if they shuffle enough paperwork and ignore enough of my calls, I'll just throw my hands up in frustration and walk away so they can do whatever they want. But I won't. I see exactly what they're doing. I see the real meaning behind every single \"lost\" file and \"rescheduled\" visit, even if they refuse to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts, feelings, or actions are being controlled or influenced by something outside of yourself, like a person, group, or force?\n\nPatient: Controlled? Like... mind control? Goodness, no. No, doctor, absolutely not. I am a fifty-nine-year-old woman who has managed an entire department of administrative support staff for over two decades. Nobody controls my thoughts but me. I mean, they might try to influence the *situation*, like I was just explaining—by hiding documents, shuffling my sister's schedules around, ignoring my calls, trying to frustrate me into giving up and walking away—but they certainly aren't controlling my mind or my actions. \n\nIf anything, I am the one in complete, absolute control of my faculties. I have to be! Every single morning I wake up at six-fifteen, I make my list, I categorize my tasks by urgency, and I execute them. I don't sit around waiting for some... some outside force or group to tell me what to do or how to feel. You see, when you've spent your career in administration, you learn very quickly that you have to be the master of your own domain. You can't let external forces dictate your actions, or the whole filing system falls apart, the protocols break down, and before you know it, you have a backlog of invoices from three quarters ago and a team that doesn't know who they report to. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel like a puppet on a string. I don't have some sort of hypnotic suggestion making me do things, or... or whatever it is people with those kinds of problems experience. My feelings are entirely my own. When I am irritated, it is because someone at that clinic failed to do their job properly. When I take action—like when I marched down to the records department to demand a physical printout of the ledger—it’s because I have reviewed the data, I’ve looked at my spreadsheets—which I color-code, by the way, red for pending discrepancies, green for resolved, though there's very little green lately—and I have made a conscious, deliberate choice to act based on the facts in front of me. \n\nNobody is pulling my strings, doctor. I am completely, one hundred percent in the driver's seat. I mean, if my thoughts were being controlled by someone else, do you really think I would have been able to put together a cross-referenced, tabbed binder of all their administrative errors? I don't think so. It takes a very clear, very independent mind to do what I'm doing. So no, I don't experience anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or suspicions that others might consider extremely strange or impossible, even if they seem completely logical to you?\n\nPatient: Extremely strange or impossible? Heavens, no. I don't believe in fairy tales, doctor. I'm a pragmatic woman. You don't survive twenty-five years—well, twenty-seven if you count my time as a junior coordinator before I went back for my bachelor's—managing an administrative department by indulging in the impossible. Everything I suspect... no, let me correct that, everything I *know* is happening with my sister's care is entirely possible, and frankly, very common if you actually know where to look. \n\nI'm not talking about, you know, government agents beaming signals through my television set or aliens or microchips in the water supply or whatever it is you're checking for. If one of my staff came to me with that kind of nonsense, I'd send them straight to HR! What I'm dealing with is basic, run-of-the-mill, bureaucratic obstruction. It's just human nature. People make mistakes, they realize they've messed up the billing codes or violated a scheduling protocol, and instead of admitting fault and facing the consequences, they try to cover it up. And then they form these little alliances to keep the person asking the hard questions—which is me—out of the loop. \n\nSo, no, there is nothing impossible about it. It’s all math, logistics, and office politics. Now, I will admit, my sister's neighbor, Susan—she means well, but she's not very sharp, bless her heart—she told me I was overthinking it. She said it was 'impossible' for a whole clinic staff to coordinate against one person just to hide some paperwork. But I just had to laugh, because I told her, 'Susan, you have clearly never seen a department head try to hide a budget deficit right before a quarterly audit!' It is very, very possible. I've seen people do much more for much less. \n\nIf anyone thinks my suspicions are strange, it’s only because they haven't looked at my documentation. They haven't done the work. I have the emails printed out. I have the call logs cross-referenced with the exact dates and times they claimed they tried to reach me, which proves they didn't. Nothing I'm saying defies the laws of physics or logic. It’s just plain, undeniable fact, meticulously cataloged. So if you're asking if I believe in things that can't possibly happen... no. I only believe in what the paper trail proves, and my paper trail is airtight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Days will go by. \n\nNow, if you ask them, or if you ask anyone else looking at this from the outside, everyone says it’s just mistakes. \"Oh, we're short-staffed, the new software system updated, it's just a clerical error, we're so sorry.\" They expect me to just swallow that excuse. But I've supervised administrative support workers for decades. I know the statistical probability of this many \"mistakes\" happening to one single patient account, and it is practically zero. \n\nI know what's really happening. I know they’re intentionally keeping information from me. It's a concerted effort. They want to keep me in the dark and try to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. They know I ask the hard questions, so they think if they make it difficult enough, if they shuffle enough paperwork and ignore enough of my calls, I'll just throw my hands up in frustration and walk away so they can do whatever they want. But I won't. I see exactly what they're doing. I see the real meaning behind every single \"lost\" file and \"rescheduled\" visit, even if they refuse to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts, feelings, or actions are being controlled or influenced by something outside of yourself, like a person, group, or force?\n\nPatient: Controlled? Like... mind control? Goodness, no. No, doctor, absolutely not. I am a fifty-nine-year-old woman who has managed an entire department of administrative support staff for over two decades. Nobody controls my thoughts but me. I mean, they might try to influence the *situation*, like I was just explaining—by hiding documents, shuffling my sister's schedules around, ignoring my calls, trying to frustrate me into giving up and walking away—but they certainly aren't controlling my mind or my actions. \n\nIf anything, I am the one in complete, absolute control of my faculties. I have to be! Every single morning I wake up at six-fifteen, I make my list, I categorize my tasks by urgency, and I execute them. I don't sit around waiting for some... some outside force or group to tell me what to do or how to feel. You see, when you've spent your career in administration, you learn very quickly that you have to be the master of your own domain. You can't let external forces dictate your actions, or the whole filing system falls apart, the protocols break down, and before you know it, you have a backlog of invoices from three quarters ago and a team that doesn't know who they report to. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel like a puppet on a string. I don't have some sort of hypnotic suggestion making me do things, or... or whatever it is people with those kinds of problems experience. My feelings are entirely my own. When I am irritated, it is because someone at that clinic failed to do their job properly. When I take action—like when I marched down to the records department to demand a physical printout of the ledger—it’s because I have reviewed the data, I’ve looked at my spreadsheets—which I color-code, by the way, red for pending discrepancies, green for resolved, though there's very little green lately—and I have made a conscious, deliberate choice to act based on the facts in front of me. \n\nNobody is pulling my strings, doctor. I am completely, one hundred percent in the driver's seat. I mean, if my thoughts were being controlled by someone else, do you really think I would have been able to put together a cross-referenced, tabbed binder of all their administrative errors? I don't think so. It takes a very clear, very independent mind to do what I'm doing. So no, I don't experience anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or suspicions that others might consider extremely strange or impossible, even if they seem completely logical to you?\n\nPatient: Extremely strange or impossible? Everything I suspect... no, let me correct that, everything I *know* is happening with my sister's care is entirely possible, and frankly, very common if you actually know where to look. \n\nI'm not talking about, you know, government agents beaming signals through my television set or aliens or microchips in the water supply or whatever it is you're checking for. If one of my staff came to me with that kind of nonsense, I'd send them straight to HR! What I'm dealing with is basic, run-of-the-mill, bureaucratic obstruction. It's just human nature. People make mistakes, they realize they've messed up the billing codes or violated a scheduling protocol, and instead of admitting fault and facing the consequences, they try to cover it up. And then they form these little alliances to keep the person asking the hard questions—which is me—out of the loop. \n\nSo, no, there is nothing impossible about it. It’s all math, logistics, and office politics. Now, I will admit, my sister's neighbor, Susan—she means well, but she's not very sharp, bless her heart—she told me I was overthinking it. She said it was 'impossible' for a whole clinic staff to coordinate against one person just to hide some paperwork. But I just had to laugh, because I told her, 'Susan, you have clearly never seen a department head try to hide a budget deficit right before a quarterly audit!' It is very, very possible. I've seen people do much more for much less. \n\nIf anyone thinks my suspicions are strange, it’s only because they haven't looked at my documentation. They haven't done the work. I have the emails printed out. I have the call logs cross-referenced with the exact dates and times they claimed they tried to reach me, which proves they didn't. Nothing I'm saying defies the laws of physics or logic. It’s just plain, undeniable fact, meticulously cataloged. So if you're asking if I believe in things that can't possibly happen... no. Well, yes, actually. That is exactly what we’ve been talking about, isn't it? Because as I was explaining earlier, this whole situation with the clinic—it is so incredibly obvious to me, just plain as day, but it seems like nobody else wants to open their eyes and look at the reality of what's happening. 

I mean, I’m sitting here telling you that for months now, I have been documenting this undeniable pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around without my consent, the bills show up with completely wrong diagnostic codes, and these people just miraculously "forget" to call me back after I leave perfectly clear, actionable messages. And what does everyone else say? What do the people on the outside, or even my own extended family, say? They say exactly what the clinic wants them to say: "Oh, it’s just mistakes. Healthcare is so complicated these days, they’re just understaffed, you're expecting too much." 

It drives me absolutely up the wall! Because they don't share my perspective, even though the evidence is sitting right there in my color-coded binder. Most people just want to believe it's simple incompetence because that's easier to swallow. But I know—I know with absolute certainty—that they are intentionally keeping information from me. It is a deliberate, calculated strategy to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. 

And it’s completely obvious to me because, as an administrative supervisor, I know what a normal error rate looks like. I know the metrics. If my team had this many "accidental" scheduling conflicts or billing discrepancies on a single account, I’d be hauling them into my office for a formal performance review before lunch. But because other people don't have my background, because they haven't spent almost three decades managing office logistics and oversight, they just accept the excuses. They don't share my belief because they don't know how to look at the data, or they're just too lazy to do the math. 

But to me? When you look at the sheer volume of "lost" paperwork and unreturned calls? It couldn't be more obvious that they are trying to force me out of the picture so they can manage my sister without my oversight. I just don't understand how anyone could look at my spreadsheets and not see exactly what I see.

I mean, it started out small, you know? Just little things. My sister's doctor appointments would suddenly get moved to a different day, and when I'd call to ask why, the receptionist would just say, \"Oh, it was a scheduling error,\" or \"We had to shuffle things around.\" But then it kept happening. And not just with her appointments. The bills started showing up wrong—charges for things we never agreed to, dates mixed up, amounts that just didn't make sense. I have a bachelor's degree, I've run an office for years, I know how these systems work. I know they are deliberately keeping information from me. They are trying to push me out of making decisions for my sister, for my own life, really. I know they’re deliberately keeping information from me. They are working together to try and push me out of making decisions for my sister, to isolate me. They want to make me look incompetent, like I can't handle her care, so they can just edge me out entirely and take control of the situation. It is a targeted, secret campaign, and I am the only one who refuses to be blind to it. It has to be intentional.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed that these people or groups have been secretly working against you?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and trace it all back, look at the calendar and the timelines, which I have, by the way, I actually have a whole binder at home where I’ve documented every single incident... it’s been going on for about 11 months now. Yes, 11 months almost exactly. Because... well, it all started right around the time my sister’s health took a sudden turn for the worse. \n\nIt was so unexpected, you know? She was doing fine, just living her life, and then suddenly she was in and out of the hospital, and someone had to step up and take charge of the situation. And since I’m the one with the administrative background, the one who knows how to manage complex schedules and deal with providers and insurance companies, it naturally fell to me to start coordinating her care. I didn't mind, of course, she is my sister and I love her, but almost as soon as I took over and started making calls, the roadblocks started. \n\nI remember the very first incident, it was a Tuesday, and I had set up a crucial specialist consultation for her, and suddenly the referral was just lost in their system. At first, I thought, okay, human error, I supervise an office, I know how short-staffed places can get. But then it was the next appointment, and the next, and the billing errors. So it’s been 11 months of this constant, uphill battle. Eleven months since I stepped in to manage her care, which is exactly when they realized I am not someone they can just brush off or intimidate. That is when they had to start coordinating these tactics to try and push me out of the picture. They saw me taking control 11 months ago, and they have been working against me in the shadows ever since.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these people or groups have a specific reason or motive for targeting you, and if so, what do you think that motive is?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, if you're asking for their specific motive... I mean, I've spent hours, literally hours, lying awake at night trying to figure out exactly *why* they chose me. Because it's not like I'm a public figure, you know? I'm just a supervisor. I run a tight ship, sure, but I'm not someone who usually attracts this kind of... focused attention. \n\nI've gone over and over it in my head. I've looked at the billing codes, I've analyzed the timelines of when they started shuffling the appointments, trying to find the missing link. Is it financial? Are they trying to cover up some sort of massive insurance fraud by making me look crazy so I can't blow the whistle? Because I *have* caught discrepancies in the billing, like I told you. Or is it more personal? Is there someone at the clinic who knows someone I fired years ago and they're holding a grudge? Or maybe they just don't like that I ask questions. You know how some doctors—present company excluded, of course—but some doctors and office managers, they just want patients who nod and say 'yes, doctor' and don't challenge them. And I challenge them. I ask for the itemized receipts. I demand to know why a specialist referral takes three weeks when the standard turnaround time in my office is 48 hours. \n\nSo, do I know their exact, specific motive? No. I don't have the smoking gun yet. I don't have the one piece of paper that says 'Target her because of X, Y, and Z.' But I know there *is* a motive. People don't coordinate this level of interference just for fun. It takes time, it takes effort to mess with someone's life this consistently. They have a reason, doctor. I just... I haven't uncovered the exact nature of it yet. But I will. I'm very thorough.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was significantly different than usual—like feeling unusually down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you normally enjoy, or feeling unusually energized, restless, or overly confident?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, I know you have to ask these things, it's part of the standard intake questionnaire, I'm very familiar with the process. But no, my mood is perfectly stable. I haven't been depressed or down in the dumps, and I certainly haven't been bouncing off the walls with energy or feeling overly confident, as you put it. \n\nHonestly, doctor, how could I possibly be depressed when I have so much work to do? I don't have the luxury of losing interest in things. I have a whole administrative team relying on me at the office, making sure the schedules are tight and the protocols are followed. And then I come home, and I have my sister's care to manage. I have the binder to update. I have the billing statements to cross-reference with the appointment logs. If anything, I'm just focused. Highly, highly focused. \n\nPeople might look at me and think, \"Oh, she's stressed, she's working too hard, she must be exhausted.\" And sure, I get tired, anyone would get tired dealing with this level of incompetence—or, well, intentional sabotage, as we've discussed. But tired is not the same as depressed. Tired is just a biological response to being the only person willing to put in the hours to uncover what's really going on. And as for feeling overly energized or restless... well, I'm thorough. I'm a thorough person. If I stay up late organizing my documentation, it's not because I'm manic or something, it's because the documentation needs to be organized. It's because if I don't do it, they win. They slip another charge past me, they cancel another appointment, and they get one step closer to pushing me out. \n\nSo, no. My mood is exactly what it should be for someone in my position. I'm alert, I'm diligent, and I'm entirely focused on the task at hand. There hasn't been any sort of extreme shift in my emotions, just a very necessary shift in my priorities to deal with this situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or sensed things that others around you don’t seem to notice, even when you were certain they were real at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, if you're asking if I'm hallucinating—seeing ghosts or hearing voices whispering in my ear—no, absolutely not. I know that's what that question means. I've read the DSM criteria, just out of curiosity, you understand. I like to be informed. But no, I don't see or hear things that aren't there. Everything I've noticed, everything I've documented in my binder, is entirely real and grounded in reality. \n\nI mean, I suppose you could say I notice things that *other* people don't seem to notice, but that's not because I'm imagining things. It's because other people aren't paying attention! People walk through life with blinders on, doctor. They just accept things at face value. A receptionist says, 'Oh, the computer system is down, we lost your appointment,' and the average person just sighs and reschedules. But I see the pattern. I see the receptionist looking away, avoiding eye contact, I see the slight hesitation before she types. I notice the way the clinic manager happens to walk by the desk exactly when I'm asking for a copy of the itemized bill. \n\nSo, do I sense things others don't? Yes, but it's a heightened sense of observation, not a... a sensory hallucination. It's my administrative training kicking in. When you spend thirty years managing an office, you learn to read people. You learn to spot the subtle cues when someone is lying to you or trying to cover their tracks. You notice when the tone of an email is just slightly off, or when a phone call is routed to a different department than it usually is. \n\nFor instance, last Thursday—or was it Wednesday? No, Thursday, because it was trash day—I was on the phone with the insurance company, and I could hear a faint clicking sound on the line. Now, most people would just think, 'Oh, a bad connection.' But I know what a secure line sounds like, and I know what interference sounds like. I didn't *imagine* the click. It was a real, physical sound. But I was the only one who recognized its significance. I was the only one who realized they were probably recording the call or monitoring my inquiries. \n\nSo, no, I don't have hallucinations. I don't see things that aren't there. I just see the things that *are* there, the things that everyone else is too busy or too naive to notice. I see the connections. And honestly, it's a good thing I do, because if I weren't paying this close attention, they would have gotten away with this months ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that certain events, people, or everyday occurrences have a special or hidden meaning specifically for you, even if others don’t see it that way?\n\nPatient: Well, \"hidden meaning\" makes it sound like I'm reading tea leaves or looking for signs in the clouds, doctor, and I'm a practical woman. I deal in facts, in logistics, in documentation. But do I believe these everyday occurrences mean something more than what people claim they do? Absolutely. Because they do. \n\nI’m here because for months now—literally months, going back to at least early fall, which I have meticulously tracked—I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern. And it is a pattern, not a coincidence. Take my sister’s care, for example. I manage everything for her, as I've mentioned. But suddenly, out of nowhere, her appointments get moved without my authorization. They’ll just change a Tuesday morning specialist slot to a Thursday afternoon and claim they sent an automated text, which, of course, I never received. And then the financial side... the bills show up wrong over and over again. Double charges for basic consultations, incorrect diagnostic codes that I have to personally audit. \n\nAnd when I call to get it sorted out? When I try to hold them accountable? People just conveniently \"forget\" to call me back. I leave detailed, professional messages with the front desk, with the billing coordinator, with the clinic manager, and it's just crickets. Days will go by. \n\nNow, if you ask them, or if you ask anyone else looking at this from the outside, everyone says it’s just mistakes. \"Oh, we're short-staffed, the new software system updated, it's just a clerical error, we're so sorry.\" They expect me to just swallow that excuse. But I've supervised administrative support workers for decades. I know the statistical probability of this many \"mistakes\" happening to one single patient account, and it is practically zero. \n\nI know what's really happening. I know they’re intentionally keeping information from me. It's a concerted effort. They want to keep me in the dark and try to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. They know I ask the hard questions, so they think if they make it difficult enough, if they shuffle enough paperwork and ignore enough of my calls, I'll just throw my hands up in frustration and walk away so they can do whatever they want. But I won't. I see exactly what they're doing. I see the real meaning behind every single \"lost\" file and \"rescheduled\" visit, even if they refuse to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts, feelings, or actions are being controlled or influenced by something outside of yourself, like a person, group, or force?\n\nPatient: Controlled? Like... mind control? Goodness, no. No, doctor, absolutely not. I am a fifty-nine-year-old woman who has managed an entire department of administrative support staff for over two decades. Nobody controls my thoughts but me. I mean, they might try to influence the *situation*, like I was just explaining—by hiding documents, shuffling my sister's schedules around, ignoring my calls, trying to frustrate me into giving up and walking away—but they certainly aren't controlling my mind or my actions. \n\nIf anything, I am the one in complete, absolute control of my faculties. I have to be! Every single morning I wake up at six-fifteen, I make my list, I categorize my tasks by urgency, and I execute them. I don't sit around waiting for some... some outside force or group to tell me what to do or how to feel. You see, when you've spent your career in administration, you learn very quickly that you have to be the master of your own domain. You can't let external forces dictate your actions, or the whole filing system falls apart, the protocols break down, and before you know it, you have a backlog of invoices from three quarters ago and a team that doesn't know who they report to. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel like a puppet on a string. I don't have some sort of hypnotic suggestion making me do things, or... or whatever it is people with those kinds of problems experience. My feelings are entirely my own. When I am irritated, it is because someone at that clinic failed to do their job properly. When I take action—like when I marched down to the records department to demand a physical printout of the ledger—it’s because I have reviewed the data, I’ve looked at my spreadsheets—which I color-code, by the way, red for pending discrepancies, green for resolved, though there's very little green lately—and I have made a conscious, deliberate choice to act based on the facts in front of me. \n\nNobody is pulling my strings, doctor. I am completely, one hundred percent in the driver's seat. I mean, if my thoughts were being controlled by someone else, do you really think I would have been able to put together a cross-referenced, tabbed binder of all their administrative errors? I don't think so. It takes a very clear, very independent mind to do what I'm doing. So no, I don't experience anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or suspicions that others might consider extremely strange or impossible, even if they seem completely logical to you?\n\nPatient: Extremely strange or impossible? Heavens, no. I don't believe in fairy tales, doctor. I'm a pragmatic woman. You don't survive twenty-five years—well, twenty-seven if you count my time as a junior coordinator before I went back for my bachelor's—managing an administrative department by indulging in the impossible. Everything I suspect... no, let me correct that, everything I *know* is happening with my sister's care is entirely possible, and frankly, very common if you actually know where to look. \n\nI'm not talking about, you know, government agents beaming signals through my television set or aliens or microchips in the water supply or whatever it is you're checking for. If one of my staff came to me with that kind of nonsense, I'd send them straight to HR! What I'm dealing with is basic, run-of-the-mill, bureaucratic obstruction. It's just human nature. People make mistakes, they realize they've messed up the billing codes or violated a scheduling protocol, and instead of admitting fault and facing the consequences, they try to cover it up. And then they form these little alliances to keep the person asking the hard questions—which is me—out of the loop. \n\nSo, no, there is nothing impossible about it. It’s all math, logistics, and office politics. Now, I will admit, my sister's neighbor, Susan—she means well, but she's not very sharp, bless her heart—she told me I was overthinking it. She said it was 'impossible' for a whole clinic staff to coordinate against one person just to hide some paperwork. But I just had to laugh, because I told her, 'Susan, you have clearly never seen a department head try to hide a budget deficit right before a quarterly audit!' It is very, very possible. I've seen people do much more for much less. \n\nIf anyone thinks my suspicions are strange, it’s only because they haven't looked at my documentation. They haven't done the work. I have the emails printed out. I have the call logs cross-referenced with the exact dates and times they claimed they tried to reach me, which proves they didn't. Nothing I'm saying defies the laws of physics or logic. It’s just plain, undeniable fact, meticulously cataloged. So if you're asking if I believe in things that can't possibly happen... no. I only believe in what the paper trail proves, and my paper trail is airtight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs about yourself or the world that you know other people don't share, even if they seem completely obvious to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That is exactly what we’ve been talking about, isn't it? Because as I was explaining earlier, this whole situation with the clinic—it is so incredibly obvious to me, just plain as day, but it seems like nobody else wants to open their eyes and look at the reality of what's happening. \n\nI mean, I’m sitting here telling you that for months now, I have been documenting this undeniable pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around without my consent, the bills show up with completely wrong diagnostic codes, and these people just miraculously \"forget\" to call me back after I leave perfectly clear, actionable messages. And what does everyone else say? What do the people on the outside, or even my own extended family, say? They say exactly what the clinic wants them to say: \"Oh, it’s just mistakes. Healthcare is so complicated these days, they’re just understaffed, you're expecting too much.\" \n\nIt drives me absolutely up the wall! Because they don't share my perspective, even though the evidence is sitting right there in my color-coded binder. Most people just want to believe it's simple incompetence because that's easier to swallow. But I know—I know with absolute certainty—that they are intentionally keeping information from me. It is a deliberate, calculated strategy to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. \n\nAnd it’s completely obvious to me because, as an administrative supervisor, I know what a normal error rate looks like. But because other people don't have my background, because they haven't spent almost three decades managing office logistics and oversight, they just accept the excuses. They don't share my belief because they don't know how to look at the data, or they're just too lazy to do the math. \n\nBut to me? When you look at the sheer volume of \"lost\" paperwork and unreturned calls? It couldn't be more obvious that they are trying to force me out of the picture so they can manage my sister without my oversight. I just don't understand how anyone could look at my spreadsheets and not see exactly what I see.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Everything I suspect... no, let me correct that, everything I *know* is happening with my sister's care is entirely possible, and frankly, very common if you actually know where to look. \n\nI'm not talking about, you know, government agents beaming signals through my television set or aliens or microchips in the water supply or whatever it is you're checking for. If one of my staff came to me with that kind of nonsense, I'd send them straight to HR! What I'm dealing with is basic, run-of-the-mill, bureaucratic obstruction. It's just human nature. People make mistakes, they realize they've messed up the billing codes or violated a scheduling protocol, and instead of admitting fault and facing the consequences, they try to cover it up. And then they form these little alliances to keep the person asking the hard questions—which is me—out of the loop. \n\nSo, no, there is nothing impossible about it. It’s all math, logistics, and office politics. Now, I will admit, my sister's neighbor, Susan—she means well, but she's not very sharp, bless her heart—she told me I was overthinking it. She said it was 'impossible' for a whole clinic staff to coordinate against one person just to hide some paperwork. But I just had to laugh, because I told her, 'Susan, you have clearly never seen a department head try to hide a budget deficit right before a quarterly audit!' It is very, very possible. I've seen people do much more for much less. \n\nIf anyone thinks my suspicions are strange, it’s only because they haven't looked at my documentation. They haven't done the work. I have the emails printed out. I have the call logs cross-referenced with the exact dates and times they claimed they tried to reach me, which proves they didn't. Nothing I'm saying defies the laws of physics or logic. It’s just plain, undeniable fact, meticulously cataloged. So if you're asking if I believe in things that can't possibly happen... no. I only believe in what the paper trail proves, and my paper trail is airtight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs about yourself or the world that you know other people don't share, even if they seem completely obvious to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That is exactly what we’ve been talking about, isn't it? Because as I was explaining earlier, this whole situation with the clinic—it is so incredibly obvious to me, just plain as day, but it seems like nobody else wants to open their eyes and look at the reality of what's happening. \n\nI mean, I’m sitting here telling you that for months now, I have been documenting this undeniable pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around without my consent, the bills show up with completely wrong diagnostic codes, and these people just miraculously \"forget\" to call me back after I leave perfectly clear, actionable messages. And what does everyone else say? What do the people on the outside, or even my own extended family, say? They say exactly what the clinic wants them to say: \"Oh, it’s just mistakes. Healthcare is so complicated these days, they’re just understaffed, you're expecting too much.\" \n\nIt drives me absolutely up the wall! Because they don't share my perspective, even though the evidence is sitting right there in my color-coded binder. Most people just want to believe it's simple incompetence because that's easier to swallow. But I know—I know with absolute certainty—that they are intentionally keeping information from me. It is a deliberate, calculated strategy to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. \n\nAnd it’s completely obvious to me because, as an administrative supervisor, I know what a normal error rate looks like. I know the metrics. If my team had this many \"accidental\" scheduling conflicts or billing discrepancies on a single account, I’d be hauling them into my office for a formal performance review before lunch. But because other people don't have my background, because they haven't spent almost three decades managing office logistics and oversight, they just accept the excuses. They don't share my belief because they don't know how to look at the data, or they're just too lazy to do the math. \n\nBut to me? When you look at the sheer volume of \"lost\" paperwork and unreturned calls? I just don't understand how anyone could look at my spreadsheets and not see exactly what I see.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Absolutely. Without a shadow of a doubt. That is... well, that’s exactly why I’m here, doctor. Because for months now—literally months, going all the way back to the start of the fourth quarter last year—I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern play out week after week. 

My sister’s appointments get moved around without any prior notification, usually to times they know I'm stuck in departmental meetings. The bills show up completely wrong, with incorrect diagnostic codes that I then have to spend hours untangling. And the staff... oh, the staff just miraculously "forget" to call me back after I leave perfectly clear, urgent messages with the front desk. "Oh, the sticky note must have fallen off my monitor," they say. Please. I haven't used a sticky note for a primary message system since 1998. 

And even when everyone else—my extended family, the neighbors, the so-called patient advocate at the clinic—even when they all sit there and tell me it’s just mistakes, just a string of unfortunate coincidences or a busy office adjusting to a new software system... I know better. You don't have this many "coincidences" in a properly managed facility. I know, with absolute certainty, that they are intentionally keeping information from me. They are deliberately trying to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. 

I mean, think about it from a purely logical standpoint. If you have someone like me, who actually reads the itemized statements, who checks the scheduling logs, who holds people accountable... well, you become a nuisance to them, don't you? They don't want someone looking over their shoulder. They want to do things their way, cut corners, and not have to answer to anyone. So they coordinate. They stonewall. They think if they frustrate me enough with these endless "mistakes," I'll just throw my hands up and let them take full control. But I won't. I absolutely won't. I've spent too many years dealing with people trying to skirt protocol to let a handful of petty office clerks push me out of my own sister's life.

They are trying to push me out of making decisions for my sister, for my own life, really. Exactly! \"In secret\" is exactly what it is. Because... well, they aren't going to just come right out and say it to my face, are they? No, it's all done behind my back, in the shadows. My sister’s appointments get moved without my authorization, and then the bills show up wrong—with completely fabricated charges and mismatched dates, I might add—and these people just conveniently \"forget\" to call me back when I leave voicemails demanding an explanation. I leave very clear, professional messages. I supervise an administrative team, doctor, I train people on phone etiquette, so I know how to leave a message that requires a follow-up! But nothing. Radio silence. \n\nAnd even when everyone around me—my friends, my family, people who should be on my side—says, \"Oh, it’s just mistakes, offices are short-staffed right now, you're being paranoid,\" I know that's not true. I know they’re deliberately keeping information from me. They are working together to try and push me out of making decisions for my sister, to isolate me. They want to make me look incompetent, like I can't handle her care, so they can just edge me out entirely and take control of the situation. It is a targeted, secret campaign, and I am the only one who refuses to be blind to it. I mean, the mathematical improbability of all these \"mistakes\" happening to one person over and over again... you see what I'm saying, right? It has to be intentional.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed that these people or groups have been secretly working against you?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and trace it all back, look at the calendar and the timelines, which I have, by the way, I actually have a whole binder at home where I’ve documented every single incident... it’s been going on for about 11 months now. Yes, 11 months almost exactly. Because... well, it all started right around the time my sister’s health took a sudden turn for the worse. \n\nIt was so unexpected, you know? She was doing fine, just living her life, and then suddenly she was in and out of the hospital, and someone had to step up and take charge of the situation. There hasn't been any sort of extreme shift in my emotions, just a very necessary shift in my priorities to deal with this situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or sensed things that others around you don’t seem to notice, even when you were certain they were real at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, if you're asking if I'm hallucinating—seeing ghosts or hearing voices whispering in my ear—no, absolutely not. I know that's what that question means. I've read the DSM criteria, just out of curiosity, you understand. I like to be informed. But no, I don't see or hear things that aren't there. Everything I've noticed, everything I've documented in my binder, is entirely real and grounded in reality. \n\nI mean, I suppose you could say I notice things that *other* people don't seem to notice, but that's not because I'm imagining things. It's because other people aren't paying attention! People walk through life with blinders on, doctor. They just accept things at face value. A receptionist says, 'Oh, the computer system is down, we lost your appointment,' and the average person just sighs and reschedules. But I see the pattern. I see the receptionist looking away, avoiding eye contact, I see the slight hesitation before she types. I notice the way the clinic manager happens to walk by the desk exactly when I'm asking for a copy of the itemized bill. \n\nSo, do I sense things others don't? Yes, but it's a heightened sense of observation, not a... a sensory hallucination. It's my administrative training kicking in. When you spend thirty years managing an office, you learn to read people. You learn to spot the subtle cues when someone is lying to you or trying to cover their tracks. You notice when the tone of an email is just slightly off, or when a phone call is routed to a different department than it usually is. \n\nFor instance, last Thursday—or was it Wednesday? No, Thursday, because it was trash day—I was on the phone with the insurance company, and I could hear a faint clicking sound on the line. Now, most people would just think, 'Oh, a bad connection.' But I know what a secure line sounds like, and I know what interference sounds like. I didn't *imagine* the click. It was a real, physical sound. But I was the only one who recognized its significance. I was the only one who realized they were probably recording the call or monitoring my inquiries. \n\nSo, no, I don't have hallucinations. I don't see things that aren't there. I just see the things that *are* there, the things that everyone else is too busy or too naive to notice. I see the connections. And honestly, it's a good thing I do, because if I weren't paying this close attention, they would have gotten away with this months ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that certain events, people, or everyday occurrences have a special or hidden meaning specifically for you, even if others don’t see it that way?\n\nPatient: Well, \"hidden meaning\" makes it sound like I'm reading tea leaves or looking for signs in the clouds, doctor, and I'm a practical woman. I deal in facts, in logistics, in documentation. But do I believe these everyday occurrences mean something more than what people claim they do? Absolutely. Because they do. \n\nI’m here because for months now—literally months, going back to at least early fall, which I have meticulously tracked—I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern. And it is a pattern, not a coincidence. Take my sister’s care, for example. I manage everything for her, as I've mentioned. But suddenly, out of nowhere, her appointments get moved without my authorization. They’ll just change a Tuesday morning specialist slot to a Thursday afternoon and claim they sent an automated text, which, of course, I never received. And then the financial side... the bills show up wrong over and over again. Double charges for basic consultations, incorrect diagnostic codes that I have to personally audit. \n\nAnd when I call to get it sorted out? When I try to hold them accountable? People just conveniently \"forget\" to call me back. I leave detailed, professional messages with the front desk, with the billing coordinator, with the clinic manager, and it's just crickets. Days will go by. \n\nNow, if you ask them, or if you ask anyone else looking at this from the outside, everyone says it’s just mistakes. \"Oh, we're short-staffed, the new software system updated, it's just a clerical error, we're so sorry.\" They expect me to just swallow that excuse. But I've supervised administrative support workers for decades. I know the statistical probability of this many \"mistakes\" happening to one single patient account, and it is practically zero. \n\nI know what's really happening. I know they’re intentionally keeping information from me. It's a concerted effort. They want to keep me in the dark and try to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. They know I ask the hard questions, so they think if they make it difficult enough, if they shuffle enough paperwork and ignore enough of my calls, I'll just throw my hands up in frustration and walk away so they can do whatever they want. But I won't. I see exactly what they're doing. I see the real meaning behind every single \"lost\" file and \"rescheduled\" visit, even if they refuse to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts, feelings, or actions are being controlled or influenced by something outside of yourself, like a person, group, or force?\n\nPatient: Controlled? Like... mind control? Goodness, no. No, doctor, absolutely not. I am a fifty-nine-year-old woman who has managed an entire department of administrative support staff for over two decades. Nobody controls my thoughts but me. I mean, they might try to influence the *situation*, like I was just explaining—by hiding documents, shuffling my sister's schedules around, ignoring my calls, trying to frustrate me into giving up and walking away—but they certainly aren't controlling my mind or my actions. \n\nIf anything, I am the one in complete, absolute control of my faculties. I have to be! Every single morning I wake up at six-fifteen, I make my list, I categorize my tasks by urgency, and I execute them. I don't sit around waiting for some... some outside force or group to tell me what to do or how to feel. You see, when you've spent your career in administration, you learn very quickly that you have to be the master of your own domain. You can't let external forces dictate your actions, or the whole filing system falls apart, the protocols break down, and before you know it, you have a backlog of invoices from three quarters ago and a team that doesn't know who they report to. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel like a puppet on a string. I don't have some sort of hypnotic suggestion making me do things, or... or whatever it is people with those kinds of problems experience. My feelings are entirely my own. When I am irritated, it is because someone at that clinic failed to do their job properly. When I take action—like when I marched down to the records department to demand a physical printout of the ledger—it’s because I have reviewed the data, I’ve looked at my spreadsheets—which I color-code, by the way, red for pending discrepancies, green for resolved, though there's very little green lately—and I have made a conscious, deliberate choice to act based on the facts in front of me. \n\nNobody is pulling my strings, doctor. I am completely, one hundred percent in the driver's seat. I mean, if my thoughts were being controlled by someone else, do you really think I would have been able to put together a cross-referenced, tabbed binder of all their administrative errors? I don't think so. It takes a very clear, very independent mind to do what I'm doing. So no, I don't experience anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs or suspicions that others might consider extremely strange or impossible, even if they seem completely logical to you?\n\nPatient: Extremely strange or impossible? Heavens, no. I don't believe in fairy tales, doctor. I'm a pragmatic woman. You don't survive twenty-five years—well, twenty-seven if you count my time as a junior coordinator before I went back for my bachelor's—managing an administrative department by indulging in the impossible. Everything I suspect... no, let me correct that, everything I *know* is happening with my sister's care is entirely possible, and frankly, very common if you actually know where to look. \n\nI'm not talking about, you know, government agents beaming signals through my television set or aliens or microchips in the water supply or whatever it is you're checking for. If one of my staff came to me with that kind of nonsense, I'd send them straight to HR! What I'm dealing with is basic, run-of-the-mill, bureaucratic obstruction. It's just human nature. People make mistakes, they realize they've messed up the billing codes or violated a scheduling protocol, and instead of admitting fault and facing the consequences, they try to cover it up. And then they form these little alliances to keep the person asking the hard questions—which is me—out of the loop. \n\nSo, no, there is nothing impossible about it. It’s all math, logistics, and office politics. Now, I will admit, my sister's neighbor, Susan—she means well, but she's not very sharp, bless her heart—she told me I was overthinking it. She said it was 'impossible' for a whole clinic staff to coordinate against one person just to hide some paperwork. But I just had to laugh, because I told her, 'Susan, you have clearly never seen a department head try to hide a budget deficit right before a quarterly audit!' It is very, very possible. I've seen people do much more for much less. \n\nIf anyone thinks my suspicions are strange, it’s only because they haven't looked at my documentation. They haven't done the work. I have the emails printed out. I have the call logs cross-referenced with the exact dates and times they claimed they tried to reach me, which proves they didn't. Nothing I'm saying defies the laws of physics or logic. It’s just plain, undeniable fact, meticulously cataloged. So if you're asking if I believe in things that can't possibly happen... no. I only believe in what the paper trail proves, and my paper trail is airtight.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs about yourself or the world that you know other people don't share, even if they seem completely obvious to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That is exactly what we’ve been talking about, isn't it? Because as I was explaining earlier, this whole situation with the clinic—it is so incredibly obvious to me, just plain as day, but it seems like nobody else wants to open their eyes and look at the reality of what's happening. \n\nI mean, I’m sitting here telling you that for months now, I have been documenting this undeniable pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around without my consent, the bills show up with completely wrong diagnostic codes, and these people just miraculously \"forget\" to call me back after I leave perfectly clear, actionable messages. And what does everyone else say? What do the people on the outside, or even my own extended family, say? They say exactly what the clinic wants them to say: \"Oh, it’s just mistakes. Healthcare is so complicated these days, they’re just understaffed, you're expecting too much.\" \n\nIt drives me absolutely up the wall! Because they don't share my perspective, even though the evidence is sitting right there in my color-coded binder. Most people just want to believe it's simple incompetence because that's easier to swallow. But I know—I know with absolute certainty—that they are intentionally keeping information from me. It is a deliberate, calculated strategy to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. \n\nAnd it’s completely obvious to me because, as an administrative supervisor, I know what a normal error rate looks like. I know the metrics. If my team had this many \"accidental\" scheduling conflicts or billing discrepancies on a single account, I’d be hauling them into my office for a formal performance review before lunch. But because other people don't have my background, because they haven't spent almost three decades managing office logistics and oversight, they just accept the excuses. They don't share my belief because they don't know how to look at the data, or they're just too lazy to do the math. \n\nBut to me? When you look at the sheer volume of \"lost\" paperwork and unreturned calls? It couldn't be more obvious that they are trying to force me out of the picture so they can manage my sister without my oversight. I just don't understand how anyone could look at my spreadsheets and not see exactly what I see.\n\nDoctor: Even though others have told you that these events are just mistakes or coincidences, do you still believe with absolute certainty that these are deliberate, coordinated actions against you?\n\nPatient: Absolutely. Without a shadow of a doubt. That is... well, that’s exactly why I’m here, doctor. Because for months now—literally months, going all the way back to the start of the fourth quarter last year—I’ve been seeing the exact same pattern play out week after week. \n\nMy sister’s appointments get moved around without any prior notification, usually to times they know I'm stuck in departmental meetings. The bills show up completely wrong, with incorrect diagnostic codes that I then have to spend hours untangling. And the staff... oh, the staff just miraculously \"forget\" to call me back after I leave perfectly clear, urgent messages with the front desk. \"Oh, the sticky note must have fallen off my monitor,\" they say. Please. I haven't used a sticky note for a primary message system since 1998. \n\nAnd even when everyone else—my extended family, the neighbors, the so-called patient advocate at the clinic—even when they all sit there and tell me it’s just mistakes, just a string of unfortunate coincidences or a busy office adjusting to a new software system... I know better. You don't have this many \"coincidences\" in a properly managed facility. I know, with absolute certainty, that they are intentionally keeping information from me. They are deliberately trying to push me out of the decisions regarding my sister's care. \n\nI mean, think about it from a purely logical standpoint. If you have someone like me, who actually reads the itemized statements, who checks the scheduling logs, who holds people accountable... well, you become a nuisance to them, don't you? They don't want someone looking over their shoulder. They want to do things their way, cut corners, and not have to answer to anyone. So they coordinate. They stonewall. They think if they frustrate me enough with these endless \"mistakes,\" I'll just throw my hands up and let them take full control. But I won't. I absolutely won't. I've spent too many years dealing with people trying to skirt protocol to let a handful of petty office clerks push me out of my own sister's life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unusually high or energetic? No, absolutely not. I am a very even-keeled person, doctor. You simply cannot manage an administrative department—especially with the kind of turnover rates we’ve been seeing over the last few years—if you are prone to emotional roller coasters. I am consistent. I come in at 7:45 every morning, I review the daily logs, I have exactly one cup of black coffee, and I get to work. Now, you mentioned being irritable. Let me be very clear about that part of it. Do I get irritated? Of course I do! But it’s not some mysterious mood that just descends on me for days at a time for no reason. I get irritated when it is entirely justified. If I ask a front desk clerk to send over a simple itemized receipt and three days later I'm still waiting, yes, I am going to be irritable. If I have to spend my entire lunch break cross-referencing billing codes because someone at the clinic was too lazy to do their job correctly the first time, yes, I will be annoyed. But that is a perfectly normal, rational reaction to gross incompetence. Anyone in my position would be irritated! It would be strange if I *wasn't* irritated by people constantly trying to pull the wool over my eyes.

But as for feeling "unusually high" or overly energetic out of nowhere? No. Never. I pace myself. I am methodical, I am organized, and I am steady. If I were running around with all this erratic energy, making impulsive decisions or acting out of character, I wouldn't have been able to compile the meticulous records I have. I wouldn't be able to catch all the little "mistakes" these people keep making. My energy levels are perfectly normal for a woman with a demanding career and a sister who requires serious advocacy. I don't have time for moods, doctor. I have work to do, and I have to stay sharp to make sure things are done right.

Down or hopeless? No. Well, I mean, honestly, doctor, who has the time for that? Hopelessness implies that you’ve given up, that you’re just letting things happen to you, and as I think I’ve made abundantly clear by now, I am absolutely not giving up. And as for my sleep and appetite... no, nothing like that. I am a creature of habit. You have to be, in my line of work. My sleep schedule is exactly what it has been for the last fifteen years. I am in bed by 10:30 PM, I read for maybe twenty or thirty minutes—usually historical biographies, I find them very grounding when the rest of the world is acting so unprofessionally—and I am asleep by 11:00. I wake up at 6:00 AM sharp, usually without an alarm. Now, sure, occasionally I might take a little longer to drift off if I’m mentally drafting an email to the clinic’s grievance department or trying to remember if I cross-referenced a specific date in my binder, but I am certainly not tossing and turning all night or sleeping the day away. 

And my appetite is perfectly normal. I meal prep on Sundays. Chicken, brown rice, a green vegetable—it’s efficient, it’s healthy, and it ensures I don't have to waste time thinking about lunch during the workweek. You don't maintain a household, advocate for a vulnerable family member, and manage a department of twelve administrative staff by skipping meals and moping around. You need fuel to keep up with all of this. So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or my routines. My routines are exactly what keep me anchored while I deal with all this... this orchestrated incompetence at the clinic. If anything, I'm more focused than I've ever been.
